I see it in every ID photo, and whenever I have to turn on the camera for my online classes. \n\nPeople tell me they don’t see it, but I know it's there. I feel like everyone in the CNC lab is noticing it before they even notice my work. It's just really getting to me.\n\nDoctor: Is it just your face that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you think look off?\n\nPatient: No, it's really just my face. I mean, my nose and my jaw specifically on the left side. I don't really have any issues with the rest of my body, it's just... that one part that I can't stop focusing on.\n\nDoctor: Is this concern about your face affecting your daily life or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it really is. Um... I've actually missed quite a few of my CNC labs and shop shifts lately. Some days I just... I couldn't even get myself out the door. It's gotten to the point where my faculty mentor told me that my work placement could be in jeopardy if I keep missing time. \n\nAnd outside of school, it's pretty much the same. I stopped playing soccer, and I used to volunteer but I dropped that too. I just... I really don't want people looking at me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything repeatedly to try to feel better about your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Um, before I leave for school, I get stuck checking my face in the bathroom mirror. And then I'll use my phone camera too, just checking different angles. I spend a lot of time fixing my hair or changing my shirt, trying to see if I can hide how crooked it looks. I usually end up asking my mom if it looks weird, too. \n\nSometimes I get stuck doing all of that for so long that I just miss my lab completely. Or if I do finally make it in, I'm just too wiped out to even focus on my work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your face in the mirror or camera a certain number of times before you can stop?\n\nPatient: No, it's not really a specific number. I don't count or anything like that. It's more just... I keep checking until I either feel like I've hidden it enough, or until I just give up because I'm so frustrated. It's not about doing it three times or ten times, it's just about trying to make it look right.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors to reduce anxiety or distress?\n\nPatient: Um... I'm not really sure if I'd call it that. I don't really think about it as trying to calm down or reduce anxiety, exactly. \n\nIt's more just... I feel like I have to know how bad it looks before I let anyone else see me. Like, I can't just walk out the door without checking, because what if it's really obvious that day? So I just... If I try to just ignore it and walk out the door, I can't. I just end up right back in front of the mirror. So I guess it's something I do myself, but I don't really feel like I have the option not to do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever worry that something bad will happen if you don't check your face?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It's just really getting to me.\n\nDoctor: Is it just your face that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you think look off?\n\nPatient: No, it's really just my face. I mean, my nose and my jaw specifically on the left side. I don't really have any issues with the rest of my body, it's just... that one part that I can't stop focusing on.\n\nDoctor: Is this concern about your face affecting your daily life or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it really is. Um... I've actually missed quite a few of my CNC labs and shop shifts lately. Some days I just... I couldn't even get myself out the door. It's gotten to the point where my faculty mentor told me that my work placement could be in jeopardy if I keep missing time. \n\nAnd outside of school, it's pretty much the same. I stopped playing soccer, and I used to volunteer but I dropped that too. I just... I really don't want people looking at me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything repeatedly to try to feel better about your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Um, before I leave for school, I get stuck checking my face in the bathroom mirror. And then I'll use my phone camera too, just checking different angles. I spend a lot of time fixing my hair or changing my shirt, trying to see if I can hide how crooked it looks. I usually end up asking my mom if it looks weird, too. \n\nSometimes I get stuck doing all of that for so long that I just miss my lab completely. Or if I do finally make it in, I'm just too wiped out to even focus on my work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your face in the mirror or camera a certain number of times before you can stop?\n\nPatient: No, it's not really a specific number. I don't count or anything like that. It's more just... I keep checking until I either feel like I've hidden it enough, or until I just give up because I'm so frustrated. It's not about doing it three times or ten times, it's just about trying to make it look right.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors to reduce anxiety or distress?\n\nPatient: Um... I'm not really sure if I'd call it that. I don't really think about it as trying to calm down or reduce anxiety, exactly. \n\nIt's more just... I feel like I have to know how bad it looks before I let anyone else see me. Like, I can't just walk out the door without checking, because what if it's really obvious that day? So I just... I do it to see if I can fix it or hide it, I guess, not really to make myself feel less anxious. Honestly, looking in the mirror usually just makes me feel worse anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're forced to check your face, or is it something you choose to do?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure how to answer that. I wouldn't say I'm forced, like by someone else or anything, but... it definitely doesn't feel like a choice, either. \n\nIt's more like I just can't leave without doing it. If I try to just ignore it and walk out the door, I can't. I just end up right back in front of the mirror. So I guess it's something I do myself, but I don't really feel like I have the option not to do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever worry that something bad will happen if you don't check your face?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't think like... like I'll get into an accident or someone will die or anything like that. It's not a superstitious thing. \n\nThe only \"bad\" thing I worry about is just... people seeing me. Seeing my face looking terrible. That's the only thing I'm afraid of happening if I don't check. It's just about how I look, not about anything else happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face actually looks different from how others see it, or is it more that you're afraid of what others might think of your appearance?\n\nPatient: I think... I think it actually looks that way. I mean, that's why I came in here to begin with. I just can't stop thinking about how the left side of my face looks completely off. \n\nLike, my nose and my jaw, they just look crooked. I see it in every ID photo I take, or whenever I have to turn my camera on for my online classes. It's just right there. And I know people tell me they don't see it, but... I don't believe them. When I'm in the CNC lab for work, I just feel like everyone is staring at it. Like they're noticing my crooked face before they even look at the work I'm doing. So, yeah, I think it really looks like that, and I'm definitely afraid of what they're thinking when they see it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I think I would. I mean, even when I'm just getting ready in the morning, before anyone else is even around, I get stuck doing it. 

Before I leave for school, I'll be in the bathroom just... checking my face in the mirror over and over. And then I'll use my phone camera to check it from different angles. I'll try fixing my hair differently or changing my shirt to see if it hides the crookedness better. I even ask my mom if it looks weird before I head out. Sometimes I get so caught up in doing all that, checking and trying to fix it, that I actually miss my lab entirely, or by the time I get there, I'm just too wiped out and stressed to even focus on my work. So yeah, it's not just when people are looking at me, it happens when I'm alone too.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. I came in because I just can’t stop thinking that the left side of my face looks... off. Like my nose and my jaw are crooked. I see it in every ID photo, and whenever I have to turn on the camera for my online classes. \n\nPeople tell me they don’t see it, but I know it's there. I feel like everyone in the CNC lab is noticing it before they even notice my work. It's just really getting to me.\n\nDoctor: Is it just your face that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you think look off?\n\nPatient: No, it's really just my face. I mean, my nose and my jaw specifically on the left side. I don't really have any issues with the rest of my body, it's just... that one part that I can't stop focusing on.\n\nDoctor: Is this concern about your face affecting your daily life or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it really is. Um... I've actually missed quite a few of my CNC labs and shop shifts lately. Some days I just... I couldn't even get myself out the door. It's gotten to the point where my faculty mentor told me that my work placement could be in jeopardy if I keep missing time. \n\nAnd outside of school, it's pretty much the same. I stopped playing soccer, and I used to volunteer but I dropped that too. I just... I really don't want people looking at me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything repeatedly to try to feel better about your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Um, before I leave for school, I get stuck checking my face in the bathroom mirror. And then I'll use my phone camera too, just checking different angles. I spend a lot of time fixing my hair or changing my shirt, trying to see if I can hide how crooked it looks. I usually end up asking my mom if it looks weird, too. \n\nSometimes I get stuck doing all of that for so long that I just miss my lab completely. Or if I do finally make it in, I'm just too wiped out to even focus on my work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your face in the mirror or camera a certain number of times before you can stop?\n\nPatient: No, it's not really a specific number. I don't count or anything like that. It's more just... I keep checking until I either feel like I've hidden it enough, or until I just give up because I'm so frustrated. It's not about doing it three times or ten times, it's just about trying to make it look right.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors to reduce anxiety or distress?\n\nPatient: Um... I'm not really sure if I'd call it that. I don't really think about it as trying to calm down or reduce anxiety, exactly. \n\nIt's more just... I feel like I have to know how bad it looks before I let anyone else see me. Like, I can't just walk out the door without checking, because what if it's really obvious that day? So I just... I do it to see if I can fix it or hide it, I guess, not really to make myself feel less anxious. Honestly, looking in the mirror usually just makes me feel worse anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're forced to check your face, or is it something you choose to do?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure how to answer that. I wouldn't say I'm forced, like by someone else or anything, but... it definitely doesn't feel like a choice, either. \n\nIt's more like I just can't leave without doing it. If I try to just ignore it and walk out the door, I can't. I just end up right back in front of the mirror. So I guess it's something I do myself, but I don't really feel like I have the option not to do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever worry that something bad will happen if you don't check your face?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't think like... like I'll get into an accident or someone will die or anything like that. It's not a superstitious thing. \n\nThe only \"bad\" thing I worry about is just... people seeing me. Seeing my face looking terrible. That's the only thing I'm afraid of happening if I don't check. It's just about how I look, not about anything else happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face actually looks different from how others see it, or is it more that you're afraid of what others might think of your appearance?\n\nPatient: I think... I think it actually looks that way. I mean, that's why I came in here to begin with. I just can't stop thinking about how the left side of my face looks completely off. \n\nLike, my nose and my jaw, they just look crooked. When I'm in the CNC lab for work, I just feel like everyone is staring at it. Like they're noticing my crooked face before they even look at the work I'm doing. So, yeah, I think it really looks like that, and I'm definitely afraid of what they're thinking when they see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people would judge you or treat you differently if they saw your face as it really is?\n\nPatient: I mean, I don't know for sure if they'd treat me differently. I haven't really had anyone, like, say anything directly to my face or treat me bad at the shop or anything. \n\nBut... I definitely think they judge me. I just assume they're looking at me and thinking about how weird and crooked my face is. So I don't know exactly how it changes the way they act toward me, but I definitely feel like they're judging me for it in their heads.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check your face if you were completely alone?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think I would. I mean, even when I'm just getting ready in the morning, before anyone else is even around, I get stuck doing it. \n\nBefore I leave for school, I'll be in the bathroom just... checking my face in the mirror over and over. I feel like everyone in the CNC lab is noticing it before they even notice my work. It's just really getting to me.\n\nDoctor: Is it just your face that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you think look off?\n\nPatient: No, it's really just my face. I mean, my nose and my jaw specifically on the left side. Um... I've actually missed quite a few of my CNC labs and shop shifts lately. Some days I just... I couldn't even get myself out the door. It's gotten to the point where my faculty mentor told me that my work placement could be in jeopardy if I keep missing time. \n\nAnd outside of school, it's pretty much the same. I stopped playing soccer, and I used to volunteer but I dropped that too. I just... I really don't want people looking at me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything repeatedly to try to feel better about your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Um, before I leave for school, I get stuck checking my face in the bathroom mirror. And then I'll use my phone camera too, just checking different angles. I spend a lot of time fixing my hair or changing my shirt, trying to see if I can hide how crooked it looks. I usually end up asking my mom if it looks weird, too. \n\nSometimes I get stuck doing all of that for so long that I just miss my lab completely. Or if I do finally make it in, I'm just too wiped out to even focus on my work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your face in the mirror or camera a certain number of times before you can stop?\n\nPatient: No, it's not really a specific number. I don't count or anything like that. It's more just... I keep checking until I either feel like I've hidden it enough, or until I just give up because I'm so frustrated. It's not about doing it three times or ten times, it's just about trying to make it look right.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors to reduce anxiety or distress?\n\nPatient: Um... I'm not really sure if I'd call it that. I don't really think about it as trying to calm down or reduce anxiety, exactly. \n\nIt's more just... I just assume they're looking at me and thinking about how weird and crooked my face is. So I don't know exactly how it changes the way they act toward me, but I definitely feel like they're judging me for it in their heads.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check your face if you were completely alone?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think I would. I mean, even when I'm just getting ready in the morning, before anyone else is even around, I get stuck doing it. \n\nBefore I leave for school, I'll be in the bathroom just... checking my face in the mirror over and over. And then I'll use my phone camera to check it from different angles. I'll try fixing my hair differently or changing my shirt to see if it hides the crookedness better. I even ask my mom if it looks weird before I head out. Sometimes I get so caught up in doing all that, checking and trying to fix it, that I actually miss my lab entirely, or by the time I get there, I'm just too wiped out and stressed to even focus on my work. I'd still know that my jaw and my nose are crooked. It just... it really bothers me that it looks so wrong. \n\nSo, I don't think the worry would just go away if other people weren't in the picture. I'm pretty sure I'd still be upset about it just because of how it looks to me.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think you would still feel distressed about your face if you were certain that it actually looked perfectly normal to others, but you personally still saw it as crooked?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 20 / computer_numerically_controlled_tool_operator_or_programmer / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Plain patient.\nA plain patient describes their symptoms and experiences in a direct and straightforward manner, providing clear and relevant answers to the doctor's questions.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It's kind of both, to be honest. I mean, the main reason I came in is because I just can’t stop thinking about how the left side of my face looks off. Whenever I see an ID photo or look at my camera for an online class, my nose and jaw just look so crooked to me, and that bothers me a lot on its own. 

But the fear of what other people think is definitely a huge part of it, too. Even when people tell me they don’t see it, I always feel like everyone in the CNC lab is noticing my face before they even notice my work. So... the crookedness itself really upsets me, but feeling like everyone else is staring at it just makes it impossible to ignore.

I feel like everyone in the CNC lab is noticing it before they even notice my work. It's just really getting to me.\n\nDoctor: Is it just your face that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you think look off?\n\nPatient: No, it's really just my face. I mean, my nose and my jaw specifically on the left side. I don't really have any issues with the rest of my body, it's just... that one part that I can't stop focusing on.\n\nDoctor: Is this concern about your face affecting your daily life or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it really is. Um... I've actually missed quite a few of my CNC labs and shop shifts lately. Some days I just... I couldn't even get myself out the door. It's gotten to the point where my faculty mentor told me that my work placement could be in jeopardy if I keep missing time. \n\nAnd outside of school, it's pretty much the same. I stopped playing soccer, and I used to volunteer but I dropped that too. I just... I really don't want people looking at me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything repeatedly to try to feel better about your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Um, before I leave for school, I get stuck checking my face in the bathroom mirror. And then I'll use my phone camera too, just checking different angles. I spend a lot of time fixing my hair or changing my shirt, trying to see if I can hide how crooked it looks. I usually end up asking my mom if it looks weird, too. \n\nSometimes I get stuck doing all of that for so long that I just miss my lab completely. Or if I do finally make it in, I'm just too wiped out to even focus on my work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your face in the mirror or camera a certain number of times before you can stop?\n\nPatient: No, it's not really a specific number. I don't count or anything like that. It's more just... I keep checking until I either feel like I've hidden it enough, or until I just give up because I'm so frustrated. It's not about doing it three times or ten times, it's just about trying to make it look right.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors to reduce anxiety or distress?\n\nPatient: Um... I'm not really sure if I'd call it that. I don't really think about it as trying to calm down or reduce anxiety, exactly. \n\nIt's more just... I feel like I have to know how bad it looks before I let anyone else see me. Like, I can't just walk out the door without checking, because what if it's really obvious that day? So I just... I do it to see if I can fix it or hide it, I guess, not really to make myself feel less anxious. Honestly, looking in the mirror usually just makes me feel worse anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're forced to check your face, or is it something you choose to do?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure how to answer that. I wouldn't say I'm forced, like by someone else or anything, but... it definitely doesn't feel like a choice, either. \n\nIt's more like I just can't leave without doing it. If I try to just ignore it and walk out the door, I can't. I just end up right back in front of the mirror. So I guess it's something I do myself, but I don't really feel like I have the option not to do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever worry that something bad will happen if you don't check your face?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't think like... like I'll get into an accident or someone will die or anything like that. It's not a superstitious thing. \n\nThe only \"bad\" thing I worry about is just... people seeing me. It's just about how I look, not about anything else happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face actually looks different from how others see it, or is it more that you're afraid of what others might think of your appearance?\n\nPatient: I think... I just assume they're looking at me and thinking about how weird and crooked my face is. I'd still know that my jaw and my nose are crooked. It just... it really bothers me that it looks so wrong. \n\nSo, I don't think the worry would just go away if other people weren't in the picture. I'm pretty sure I'd still be upset about it just because of how it looks to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel distressed about your face if you were certain that it actually looked perfectly normal to others, but you personally still saw it as crooked?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think I definitely would. I mean, even if you told me everyone else thought I looked fine, I'm the one who has to look in the mirror, you know? \n\nIf I still see my jaw and nose all messed up and crooked, that's what's going to bother me. It's not just about what other people think. It's about the fact that I can see it, and it feels so wrong to me. So, yeah, I'd still be really upset and stressed out about it, even if everyone else said it was normal.\n\nDoctor: Is it the fact that you think your face looks crooked that bothers you, or is it more the fear of what others might think when they see you?\n\nPatient: It's kind of both, to be honest. I mean, the main reason I came in is because I just can’t stop thinking about how the left side of my face looks off. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don't know if I'd say I'm trying to make it perfect. It's more like... I'm just trying to hide it, or make it look less wrong. 

Like, before I leave for school, I get completely stuck checking my face in the bathroom mirror, and then I'll check my phone camera, too. I'll keep fixing my hair or changing my shirts just to try and hide the angle. I'll even ask my mom if it looks weird. I just get stuck doing it for so long that sometimes I actually miss my lab entirely. Or, if I do make it, I show up so wiped out from all the checking that I can't even focus on my work.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. I came in because I just can’t stop thinking that the left side of my face looks... off. Like my nose and my jaw are crooked. I see it in every ID photo, and whenever I have to turn on the camera for my online classes. \n\nPeople tell me they don’t see it, but I know it's there. I feel like everyone in the CNC lab is noticing it before they even notice my work. It's just really getting to me.\n\nDoctor: Is it just your face that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you think look off?\n\nPatient: No, it's really just my face. I mean, my nose and my jaw specifically on the left side. I don't really have any issues with the rest of my body, it's just... that one part that I can't stop focusing on.\n\nDoctor: Is this concern about your face affecting your daily life or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it really is. Um... I've actually missed quite a few of my CNC labs and shop shifts lately. Some days I just... I couldn't even get myself out the door. It's gotten to the point where my faculty mentor told me that my work placement could be in jeopardy if I keep missing time. \n\nAnd outside of school, it's pretty much the same. I stopped playing soccer, and I used to volunteer but I dropped that too. I just... I really don't want people looking at me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything repeatedly to try to feel better about your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Um, before I leave for school, I get stuck checking my face in the bathroom mirror. And then I'll use my phone camera too, just checking different angles. I spend a lot of time fixing my hair or changing my shirt, trying to see if I can hide how crooked it looks. I usually end up asking my mom if it looks weird, too. \n\nSometimes I get stuck doing all of that for so long that I just miss my lab completely. Or if I do finally make it in, I'm just too wiped out to even focus on my work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your face in the mirror or camera a certain number of times before you can stop?\n\nPatient: No, it's not really a specific number. I don't count or anything like that. It's more just... I keep checking until I either feel like I've hidden it enough, or until I just give up because I'm so frustrated. It's not about doing it three times or ten times, it's just about trying to make it look right.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors to reduce anxiety or distress?\n\nPatient: Um... I'm not really sure if I'd call it that. I don't really think about it as trying to calm down or reduce anxiety, exactly. \n\nIt's more just... I feel like I have to know how bad it looks before I let anyone else see me. Like, I can't just walk out the door without checking, because what if it's really obvious that day? So I just... I do it to see if I can fix it or hide it, I guess, not really to make myself feel less anxious. Honestly, looking in the mirror usually just makes me feel worse anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're forced to check your face, or is it something you choose to do?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure how to answer that. I wouldn't say I'm forced, like by someone else or anything, but... it definitely doesn't feel like a choice, either. \n\nIt's more like I just can't leave without doing it. If I try to just ignore it and walk out the door, I can't. I just end up right back in front of the mirror. So I guess it's something I do myself, but I don't really feel like I have the option not to do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever worry that something bad will happen if you don't check your face?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't think like... like I'll get into an accident or someone will die or anything like that. It's not a superstitious thing. \n\nThe only \"bad\" thing I worry about is just... people seeing me. Seeing my face looking terrible. That's the only thing I'm afraid of happening if I don't check. It's just about how I look, not about anything else happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face actually looks different from how others see it, or is it more that you're afraid of what others might think of your appearance?\n\nPatient: I think... I think it actually looks that way. I mean, that's why I came in here to begin with. I just can't stop thinking about how the left side of my face looks completely off. \n\nLike, my nose and my jaw, they just look crooked. I see it in every ID photo I take, or whenever I have to turn my camera on for my online classes. It's just right there. And I know people tell me they don't see it, but... I don't believe them. When I'm in the CNC lab for work, I just feel like everyone is staring at it. Like they're noticing my crooked face before they even look at the work I'm doing. So, yeah, I think it really looks like that, and I'm definitely afraid of what they're thinking when they see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people would judge you or treat you differently if they saw your face as it really is?\n\nPatient: I mean, I don't know for sure if they'd treat me differently. I haven't really had anyone, like, say anything directly to my face or treat me bad at the shop or anything. \n\nBut... I definitely think they judge me. I just assume they're looking at me and thinking about how weird and crooked my face is. So I don't know exactly how it changes the way they act toward me, but I definitely feel like they're judging me for it in their heads.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check your face if you were completely alone?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think I would. I mean, even when I'm just getting ready in the morning, before anyone else is even around, I get stuck doing it. \n\nBefore I leave for school, I'll be in the bathroom just... checking my face in the mirror over and over. And then I'll use my phone camera to check it from different angles. I'll try fixing my hair differently or changing my shirt to see if it hides the crookedness better. I even ask my mom if it looks weird before I head out. Sometimes I get so caught up in doing all that, checking and trying to fix it, that I actually miss my lab entirely, or by the time I get there, I'm just too wiped out and stressed to even focus on my work. So yeah, it's not just when people are looking at me, it happens when I'm alone too.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still worry about your face if you were certain that no one else could see you or judge you?\n\nPatient: Honestly... yeah, I think I still would. \n\nEven if I knew for a fact that nobody else was ever going to see me or judge me, *I* still have to see it. I'm pretty sure I'd still be upset about it just because of how it looks to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel distressed about your face if you were certain that it actually looked perfectly normal to others, but you personally still saw it as crooked?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think I definitely would. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. I came in because I just can’t stop thinking that the left side of my face looks... off. Like my nose and my jaw are crooked. I see it in every ID photo, and whenever I have to turn on the camera for my online classes. \n\nPeople tell me they don’t see it, but I know it's there. I feel like everyone in the CNC lab is noticing it before they even notice my work. It's just really getting to me.\n\nDoctor: Is it just your face that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you think look off?\n\nPatient: No, it's really just my face. I mean, my nose and my jaw specifically on the left side. I don't really have any issues with the rest of my body, it's just... that one part that I can't stop focusing on.\n\nDoctor: Is this concern about your face affecting your daily life or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it really is. Um... I've actually missed quite a few of my CNC labs and shop shifts lately. Some days I just... I couldn't even get myself out the door. It's gotten to the point where my faculty mentor told me that my work placement could be in jeopardy if I keep missing time. \n\nAnd outside of school, it's pretty much the same. I stopped playing soccer, and I used to volunteer but I dropped that too. I just... I really don't want people looking at me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything repeatedly to try to feel better about your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Um, before I leave for school, I get stuck checking my face in the bathroom mirror. And then I'll use my phone camera too, just checking different angles. I spend a lot of time fixing my hair or changing my shirt, trying to see if I can hide how crooked it looks. I usually end up asking my mom if it looks weird, too. \n\nSometimes I get stuck doing all of that for so long that I just miss my lab completely. Or if I do finally make it in, I'm just too wiped out to even focus on my work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your face in the mirror or camera a certain number of times before you can stop?\n\nPatient: No, it's not really a specific number. I don't count or anything like that. It's more just... I keep checking until I either feel like I've hidden it enough, or until I just give up because I'm so frustrated. It's not about doing it three times or ten times, it's just about trying to make it look right.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors to reduce anxiety or distress?\n\nPatient: Um... I'm not really sure if I'd call it that. I don't really think about it as trying to calm down or reduce anxiety, exactly. \n\nIt's more just... I feel like I have to know how bad it looks before I let anyone else see me. Like, I can't just walk out the door without checking, because what if it's really obvious that day? So I just... I do it to see if I can fix it or hide it, I guess, not really to make myself feel less anxious. Honestly, looking in the mirror usually just makes me feel worse anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're forced to check your face, or is it something you choose to do?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure how to answer that. I wouldn't say I'm forced, like by someone else or anything, but... it definitely doesn't feel like a choice, either. \n\nIt's more like I just can't leave without doing it. If I try to just ignore it and walk out the door, I can't. I just end up right back in front of the mirror. So I guess it's something I do myself, but I don't really feel like I have the option not to do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever worry that something bad will happen if you don't check your face?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't think like... like I'll get into an accident or someone will die or anything like that. It's not a superstitious thing. \n\nThe only \"bad\" thing I worry about is just... people seeing me. Seeing my face looking terrible. That's the only thing I'm afraid of happening if I don't check. It's just about how I look, not about anything else happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face actually looks different from how others see it, or is it more that you're afraid of what others might think of your appearance?\n\nPatient: I think... I think it actually looks that way. I mean, that's why I came in here to begin with. I just can't stop thinking about how the left side of my face looks completely off. \n\nLike, my nose and my jaw, they just look crooked. So, yeah, I think it really looks like that, and I'm definitely afraid of what they're thinking when they see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people would judge you or treat you differently if they saw your face as it really is?\n\nPatient: I mean, I don't know for sure if they'd treat me differently. I haven't really had anyone, like, say anything directly to my face or treat me bad at the shop or anything. \n\nBut... I definitely think they judge me. I just assume they're looking at me and thinking about how weird and crooked my face is. I mean, even when I'm just getting ready in the morning, before anyone else is even around, I get stuck doing it. \n\nBefore I leave for school, I'll be in the bathroom just... checking my face in the mirror over and over. I think... well, like I said earlier, it's really both. I guess the idea of having this flaw on my face is what starts the whole thing. It just bothers me so much to look at it and know it's there. 

But the distress definitely gets worse when I think about other people looking at me. Like, I know they're evaluating me when they see my face. So it's the flaw itself that upsets me, but the thought of everyone else judging me for it is what makes it so hard to deal with.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. I came in because I just can’t stop thinking that the left side of my face looks... off. Like my nose and my jaw are crooked. I see it in every ID photo, and whenever I have to turn on the camera for my online classes. \n\nPeople tell me they don’t see it, but I know it's there. I feel like everyone in the CNC lab is noticing it before they even notice my work. It's just really getting to me.\n\nDoctor: Is it just your face that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you think look off?\n\nPatient: No, it's really just my face. I mean, my nose and my jaw specifically on the left side. I don't really have any issues with the rest of my body, it's just... that one part that I can't stop focusing on.\n\nDoctor: Is this concern about your face affecting your daily life or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it really is. Um... I've actually missed quite a few of my CNC labs and shop shifts lately. Some days I just... I couldn't even get myself out the door. It's gotten to the point where my faculty mentor told me that my work placement could be in jeopardy if I keep missing time. \n\nAnd outside of school, it's pretty much the same. I stopped playing soccer, and I used to volunteer but I dropped that too. I just... I really don't want people looking at me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything repeatedly to try to feel better about your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Um, before I leave for school, I get stuck checking my face in the bathroom mirror. And then I'll use my phone camera too, just checking different angles. I spend a lot of time fixing my hair or changing my shirt, trying to see if I can hide how crooked it looks. I usually end up asking my mom if it looks weird, too. \n\nSometimes I get stuck doing all of that for so long that I just miss my lab completely. Or if I do finally make it in, I'm just too wiped out to even focus on my work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your face in the mirror or camera a certain number of times before you can stop?\n\nPatient: No, it's not really a specific number. I don't count or anything like that. It's more just... I keep checking until I either feel like I've hidden it enough, or until I just give up because I'm so frustrated. It's not about doing it three times or ten times, it's just about trying to make it look right.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors to reduce anxiety or distress?\n\nPatient: Um... I'm not really sure if I'd call it that. I don't really think about it as trying to calm down or reduce anxiety, exactly. \n\nIt's more just... I feel like I have to know how bad it looks before I let anyone else see me. Like, I can't just walk out the door without checking, because what if it's really obvious that day? So I just... I do it to see if I can fix it or hide it, I guess, not really to make myself feel less anxious. Honestly, looking in the mirror usually just makes me feel worse anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're forced to check your face, or is it something you choose to do?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure how to answer that. I wouldn't say I'm forced, like by someone else or anything, but... it definitely doesn't feel like a choice, either. \n\nIt's more like I just can't leave without doing it. If I try to just ignore it and walk out the door, I can't. I just end up right back in front of the mirror. So I guess it's something I do myself, but I don't really feel like I have the option not to do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever worry that something bad will happen if you don't check your face?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't think like... like I'll get into an accident or someone will die or anything like that. It's not a superstitious thing. \n\nThe only \"bad\" thing I worry about is just... people seeing me. Seeing my face looking terrible. That's the only thing I'm afraid of happening if I don't check. It's just about how I look, not about anything else happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face actually looks different from how others see it, or is it more that you're afraid of what others might think of your appearance?\n\nPatient: I think... I think it actually looks that way. I mean, that's why I came in here to begin with. I just can't stop thinking about how the left side of my face looks completely off. \n\nLike, my nose and my jaw, they just look crooked. I see it in every ID photo I take, or whenever I have to turn my camera on for my online classes. It's just right there. And I know people tell me they don't see it, but... I don't believe them. When I'm in the CNC lab for work, I just feel like everyone is staring at it. Like they're noticing my crooked face before they even look at the work I'm doing. So, yeah, I think it really looks like that, and I'm definitely afraid of what they're thinking when they see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people would judge you or treat you differently if they saw your face as it really is?\n\nPatient: I mean, I don't know for sure if they'd treat me differently. I haven't really had anyone, like, say anything directly to my face or treat me bad at the shop or anything. \n\nBut... I definitely think they judge me. I just assume they're looking at me and thinking about how weird and crooked my face is. So I don't know exactly how it changes the way they act toward me, but I definitely feel like they're judging me for it in their heads.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check your face if you were completely alone?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think I would. I mean, even when I'm just getting ready in the morning, before anyone else is even around, I get stuck doing it. \n\nBefore I leave for school, I'll be in the bathroom just... checking my face in the mirror over and over. And then I'll use my phone camera to check it from different angles. I'll try fixing my hair differently or changing my shirt to see if it hides the crookedness better. I even ask my mom if it looks weird before I head out. Sometimes I get so caught up in doing all that, checking and trying to fix it, that I actually miss my lab entirely, or by the time I get there, I'm just too wiped out and stressed to even focus on my work. So yeah, it's not just when people are looking at me, it happens when I'm alone too.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still worry about your face if you were certain that no one else could see you or judge you?\n\nPatient: Honestly... yeah, I think I still would. \n\nEven if I knew for a fact that nobody else was ever going to see me or judge me, *I* still have to see it. I'd still know that my jaw and my nose are crooked. It just... it really bothers me that it looks so wrong. \n\nSo, I don't think the worry would just go away if other people weren't in the picture. I'm pretty sure I'd still be upset about it just because of how it looks to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel distressed about your face if you were certain that it actually looked perfectly normal to others, but you personally still saw it as crooked?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think I definitely would. I mean, even if you told me everyone else thought I looked fine, I'm the one who has to look in the mirror, you know? \n\nIf I still see my jaw and nose all messed up and crooked, that's what's going to bother me. It's not just about what other people think. It's about the fact that I can see it, and it feels so wrong to me. So, yeah, I'd still be really upset and stressed out about it, even if everyone else said it was normal.\n\nDoctor: Is it the fact that you think your face looks crooked that bothers you, or is it more the fear of what others might think when they see you?\n\nPatient: It's kind of both, to be honest. I mean, the main reason I came in is because I just can’t stop thinking about how the left side of my face looks off. Whenever I see an ID photo or look at my camera for an online class, my nose and jaw just look so crooked to me, and that bothers me a lot on its own. \n\nBut the fear of what other people think is definitely a huge part of it, too. Even when people tell me they don’t see it, I always feel like everyone in the CNC lab is noticing my face before they even notice my work. So... the crookedness itself really upsets me, but feeling like everyone else is staring at it just makes it impossible to ignore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking behaviors are trying to make your face look perfect or exactly symmetrical?\n\nPatient: I don't know if I'd say I'm trying to make it perfect. It's more like... I'm just trying to hide it, or make it look less wrong. \n\nLike, before I leave for school, I get completely stuck checking my face in the bathroom mirror, and then I'll check my phone camera, too. I just get stuck doing it for so long that sometimes I actually miss my lab entirely. I mean, the main reason I came in is because I just can’t stop thinking about how the left side of my face looks off. Whenever I see an ID photo or look at my camera for an online class, my nose and jaw just look so crooked to me, and that bothers me a lot on its own. \n\nBut the fear of what other people think is definitely a huge part of it, too. Even when people tell me they don’t see it, I always feel like everyone in the CNC lab is noticing my face before they even notice my work. So... the crookedness itself really upsets me, but feeling like everyone else is staring at it just makes it impossible to ignore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking behaviors are trying to make your face look perfect or exactly symmetrical?\n\nPatient: I don't know if I'd say I'm trying to make it perfect. It's more like... I'm just trying to hide it, or make it look less wrong. \n\nLike, before I leave for school, I get completely stuck checking my face in the bathroom mirror, and then I'll check my phone camera, too. I'll keep fixing my hair or changing my shirts just to try and hide the angle. I'll even ask my mom if it looks weird. I just get stuck doing it for so long that sometimes I actually miss my lab entirely. Or, if I do make it, I show up so wiped out from all the checking that I can't even focus on my work.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to fix a specific flaw, like the crookedness you mentioned, or do you want your face to look flawless in general?\n\nPatient: Um, it's really just that specific thing. I'm not trying to look like a model or have a flawless face in general. I just... I just want the left side of my face to not look so messed up. \n\nIf my jaw and nose just looked normal, I wouldn't be doing all this checking and hiding. So, yeah, it's definitely about that one specific flaw, not about being perfect overall.\n\nDoctor: Is it the idea of having a flawed face that distresses you, or is it the thought of others evaluating you based on your appearance?\n\nPatient: I think... well, like I said earlier, it's really both. I guess the idea of having this flaw on my face is what starts the whole thing. It just bothers me so much to look at it and know it's there. \n\nBut the distress definitely gets worse when I think about other people looking at me. Like, I know they're evaluating me when they see my face. So it's the flaw itself that upsets me, but the thought of everyone else judging me for it is what makes it so hard to deal with.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. I came in because I just can’t stop thinking that the left side of my face looks... off. Like my nose and my jaw are crooked. I see it in every ID photo, and whenever I have to turn on the camera for my online classes. \n\nPeople tell me they don’t see it, but I know it's there. I feel like everyone in the CNC lab is noticing it before they even notice my work. It's just really getting to me.\n\nDoctor: Is it just your face that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you think look off?\n\nPatient: No, it's really just my face. I mean, my nose and my jaw specifically on the left side. I don't really have any issues with the rest of my body, it's just... that one part that I can't stop focusing on.\n\nDoctor: Is this concern about your face affecting your daily life or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it really is. Um... I've actually missed quite a few of my CNC labs and shop shifts lately. Some days I just... I couldn't even get myself out the door. It's gotten to the point where my faculty mentor told me that my work placement could be in jeopardy if I keep missing time. \n\nAnd outside of school, it's pretty much the same. I stopped playing soccer, and I used to volunteer but I dropped that too. I just... I really don't want people looking at me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything repeatedly to try to feel better about your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Um, before I leave for school, I get stuck checking my face in the bathroom mirror. And then I'll use my phone camera too, just checking different angles. I spend a lot of time fixing my hair or changing my shirt, trying to see if I can hide how crooked it looks. I usually end up asking my mom if it looks weird, too. \n\nSometimes I get stuck doing all of that for so long that I just miss my lab completely. Or if I do finally make it in, I'm just too wiped out to even focus on my work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your face in the mirror or camera a certain number of times before you can stop?\n\nPatient: No, it's not really a specific number. I don't count or anything like that. It's more just... I keep checking until I either feel like I've hidden it enough, or until I just give up because I'm so frustrated. It's not about doing it three times or ten times, it's just about trying to make it look right.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors to reduce anxiety or distress?\n\nPatient: Um... I'm not really sure if I'd call it that. I don't really think about it as trying to calm down or reduce anxiety, exactly. \n\nIt's more just... I feel like I have to know how bad it looks before I let anyone else see me. Like, I can't just walk out the door without checking, because what if it's really obvious that day? So I just... I do it to see if I can fix it or hide it, I guess, not really to make myself feel less anxious. Honestly, looking in the mirror usually just makes me feel worse anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're forced to check your face, or is it something you choose to do?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure how to answer that. I wouldn't say I'm forced, like by someone else or anything, but... it definitely doesn't feel like a choice, either. \n\nIt's more like I just can't leave without doing it. If I try to just ignore it and walk out the door, I can't. I just end up right back in front of the mirror. So I guess it's something I do myself, but I don't really feel like I have the option not to do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever worry that something bad will happen if you don't check your face?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't think like... like I'll get into an accident or someone will die or anything like that. It's not a superstitious thing. \n\nThe only \"bad\" thing I worry about is just... people seeing me. Seeing my face looking terrible. That's the only thing I'm afraid of happening if I don't check. It's just about how I look, not about anything else happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face actually looks different from how others see it, or is it more that you're afraid of what others might think of your appearance?\n\nPatient: I think... I think it actually looks that way. I mean, that's why I came in here to begin with. I just can't stop thinking about how the left side of my face looks completely off. \n\nLike, my nose and my jaw, they just look crooked. I see it in every ID photo I take, or whenever I have to turn my camera on for my online classes. It's just right there. And I know people tell me they don't see it, but... I don't believe them. When I'm in the CNC lab for work, I just feel like everyone is staring at it. Like they're noticing my crooked face before they even look at the work I'm doing. So, yeah, I think it really looks like that, and I'm definitely afraid of what they're thinking when they see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people would judge you or treat you differently if they saw your face as it really is?\n\nPatient: I mean, I don't know for sure if they'd treat me differently. I haven't really had anyone, like, say anything directly to my face or treat me bad at the shop or anything. \n\nBut... I definitely think they judge me. I just assume they're looking at me and thinking about how weird and crooked my face is. So I don't know exactly how it changes the way they act toward me, but I definitely feel like they're judging me for it in their heads.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check your face if you were completely alone?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think I would. I mean, even when I'm just getting ready in the morning, before anyone else is even around, I get stuck doing it. \n\nBefore I leave for school, I'll be in the bathroom just... checking my face in the mirror over and over. And then I'll use my phone camera to check it from different angles. I'll try fixing my hair differently or changing my shirt to see if it hides the crookedness better. I even ask my mom if it looks weird before I head out. Sometimes I get so caught up in doing all that, checking and trying to fix it, that I actually miss my lab entirely, or by the time I get there, I'm just too wiped out and stressed to even focus on my work. So yeah, it's not just when people are looking at me, it happens when I'm alone too.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still worry about your face if you were certain that no one else could see you or judge you?\n\nPatient: Honestly... yeah, I think I still would. \n\nEven if I knew for a fact that nobody else was ever going to see me or judge me, *I* still have to see it. I'd still know that my jaw and my nose are crooked. It just... it really bothers me that it looks so wrong. \n\nSo, I don't think the worry would just go away if other people weren't in the picture. I'm pretty sure I'd still be upset about it just because of how it looks to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel distressed about your face if you were certain that it actually looked perfectly normal to others, but you personally still saw it as crooked?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think I definitely would. I mean, even if you told me everyone else thought I looked fine, I'm the one who has to look in the mirror, you know? \n\nIf I still see my jaw and nose all messed up and crooked, that's what's going to bother me. Seeing my face looking terrible. That's the only thing I'm afraid of happening if I don't check. It's just about how I look, not about anything else happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face actually looks different from how others see it, or is it more that you're afraid of what others might think of your appearance?\n\nPatient: I think... I think it actually looks that way. I mean, that's why I came in here to begin with. I just can't stop thinking about how the left side of my face looks completely off. \n\nLike, my nose and my jaw, they just look crooked. I see it in every ID photo I take, or whenever I have to turn my camera on for my online classes. It's just right there. And I know people tell me they don't see it, but... I don't believe them. When I'm in the CNC lab for work, I just feel like everyone is staring at it. Like they're noticing my crooked face before they even look at the work I'm doing. So, yeah, I think it really looks like that, and I'm definitely afraid of what they're thinking when they see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people would judge you or treat you differently if they saw your face as it really is?\n\nPatient: I mean, I don't know for sure if they'd treat me differently. I haven't really had anyone, like, say anything directly to my face or treat me bad at the shop or anything. \n\nBut... I definitely think they judge me. I just assume they're looking at me and thinking about how weird and crooked my face is. So I don't know exactly how it changes the way they act toward me, but I definitely feel like they're judging me for it in their heads.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check your face if you were completely alone?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think I would. I mean, even when I'm just getting ready in the morning, before anyone else is even around, I get stuck doing it. \n\nBefore I leave for school, I'll be in the bathroom just... checking my face in the mirror over and over. And then I'll use my phone camera to check it from different angles. I'll try fixing my hair differently or changing my shirt to see if it hides the crookedness better. I even ask my mom if it looks weird before I head out. Sometimes I get so caught up in doing all that, checking and trying to fix it, that I actually miss my lab entirely, or by the time I get there, I'm just too wiped out and stressed to even focus on my work. So yeah, it's not just when people are looking at me, it happens when I'm alone too.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still worry about your face if you were certain that no one else could see you or judge you?\n\nPatient: Honestly... yeah, I think I still would. \n\nEven if I knew for a fact that nobody else was ever going to see me or judge me, *I* still have to see it. I'd still know that my jaw and my nose are crooked. It just... it really bothers me that it looks so wrong. \n\nSo, I don't think the worry would just go away if other people weren't in the picture. I'm pretty sure I'd still be upset about it just because of how it looks to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel distressed about your face if you were certain that it actually looked perfectly normal to others, but you personally still saw it as crooked?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think I definitely would. I mean, even if you told me everyone else thought I looked fine, I'm the one who has to look in the mirror, you know? \n\nIf I still see my jaw and nose all messed up and crooked, that's what's going to bother me. It's not just about what other people think. It's about the fact that I can see it, and it feels so wrong to me. So, yeah, I'd still be really upset and stressed out about it, even if everyone else said it was normal.\n\nDoctor: Is it the fact that you think your face looks crooked that bothers you, or is it more the fear of what others might think when they see you?\n\nPatient: It's kind of both, to be honest. I mean, the main reason I came in is because I just can’t stop thinking about how the left side of my face looks off. Whenever I see an ID photo or look at my camera for an online class, my nose and jaw just look so crooked to me, and that bothers me a lot on its own. \n\nBut the fear of what other people think is definitely a huge part of it, too. Even when people tell me they don’t see it, I always feel like everyone in the CNC lab is noticing my face before they even notice my work. So... the crookedness itself really upsets me, but feeling like everyone else is staring at it just makes it impossible to ignore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking behaviors are trying to make your face look perfect or exactly symmetrical?\n\nPatient: I don't know if I'd say I'm trying to make it perfect. It's more like... I'm just trying to hide it, or make it look less wrong. \n\nLike, before I leave for school, I get completely stuck checking my face in the bathroom mirror, and then I'll check my phone camera, too. I'll keep fixing my hair or changing my shirts just to try and hide the angle. I'll even ask my mom if it looks weird. I just get stuck doing it for so long that sometimes I actually miss my lab entirely. Or, if I do make it, I show up so wiped out from all the checking that I can't even focus on my work.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to fix a specific flaw, like the crookedness you mentioned, or do you want your face to look flawless in general?\n\nPatient: Um, it's really just that specific thing. I'm not trying to look like a model or have a flawless face in general. I just... I just want the left side of my face to not look so messed up. \n\nIf my jaw and nose just looked normal, I wouldn't be doing all this checking and hiding. So, yeah, it's definitely about that one specific flaw, not about being perfect overall.\n\nDoctor: Is it the idea of having a flawed face that distresses you, or is it the thought of others evaluating you based on your appearance?\n\nPatient: I think... well, like I said earlier, it's really both. I guess the idea of having this flaw on my face is what starts the whole thing. It just bothers me so much to look at it and know it's there. \n\nBut the distress definitely gets worse when I think about other people looking at me. Like, I know they're evaluating me when they see my face. So it's the flaw itself that upsets me, but the thought of everyone else judging me for it is what makes it so hard to deal with.\n\nDoctor: Is it the fact that you believe your face is flawed that causes you distress, or is it the fear that others will negatively evaluate you because of your appearance?\n\nPatient: I mean... I think I just answered that, didn't I? It's really both things. The flaw itself bothers me a lot, just knowing it's there. But the fear of other people judging me for it is definitely what makes the distress so bad. So, yeah, it's both.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n20\n\n[Current Candidates]\nF45.2, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. I came in because I just can’t stop thinking that the left side of my face looks... off. Like my nose and my jaw are crooked. I see it in every ID photo, and whenever I have to turn on the camera for my online classes. \n\nPeople tell me they don’t see it, but I know it's there. I feel like everyone in the CNC lab is noticing it before they even notice my work. It's just really getting to me.\n\nDoctor: Is it just your face that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you think look off?\n\nPatient: No, it's really just my face. I mean, my nose and my jaw specifically on the left side. I don't really have any issues with the rest of my body, it's just... that one part that I can't stop focusing on.\n\nDoctor: Is this concern about your face affecting your daily life or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it really is. Um... I've actually missed quite a few of my CNC labs and shop shifts lately. Some days I just... I couldn't even get myself out the door. It's gotten to the point where my faculty mentor told me that my work placement could be in jeopardy if I keep missing time. \n\nAnd outside of school, it's pretty much the same. I stopped playing soccer, and I used to volunteer but I dropped that too. I just... I really don't want people looking at me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything repeatedly to try to feel better about your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Um, before I leave for school, I get stuck checking my face in the bathroom mirror. And then I'll use my phone camera too, just checking different angles. I spend a lot of time fixing my hair or changing my shirt, trying to see if I can hide how crooked it looks. I usually end up asking my mom if it looks weird, too. \n\nSometimes I get stuck doing all of that for so long that I just miss my lab completely. Or if I do finally make it in, I'm just too wiped out to even focus on my work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your face in the mirror or camera a certain number of times before you can stop?\n\nPatient: No, it's not really a specific number. I don't count or anything like that. It's more just... I keep checking until I either feel like I've hidden it enough, or until I just give up because I'm so frustrated. It's not about doing it three times or ten times, it's just about trying to make it look right.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors to reduce anxiety or distress?\n\nPatient: Um... I'm not really sure if I'd call it that. I don't really think about it as trying to calm down or reduce anxiety, exactly. \n\nIt's more just... I feel like I have to know how bad it looks before I let anyone else see me. Like, I can't just walk out the door without checking, because what if it's really obvious that day? So I just... I do it to see if I can fix it or hide it, I guess, not really to make myself feel less anxious. Honestly, looking in the mirror usually just makes me feel worse anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're forced to check your face, or is it something you choose to do?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure how to answer that. I wouldn't say I'm forced, like by someone else or anything, but... it definitely doesn't feel like a choice, either. \n\nIt's more like I just can't leave without doing it. If I try to just ignore it and walk out the door, I can't. I just end up right back in front of the mirror. So I guess it's something I do myself, but I don't really feel like I have the option not to do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever worry that something bad will happen if you don't check your face?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't think like... like I'll get into an accident or someone will die or anything like that. It's not a superstitious thing. \n\nThe only \"bad\" thing I worry about is just... people seeing me. Seeing my face looking terrible. That's the only thing I'm afraid of happening if I don't check. It's just about how I look, not about anything else happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face actually looks different from how others see it, or is it more that you're afraid of what others might think of your appearance?\n\nPatient: I think... I think it actually looks that way. I mean, that's why I came in here to begin with. I just can't stop thinking about how the left side of my face looks completely off. \n\nLike, my nose and my jaw, they just look crooked. I see it in every ID photo I take, or whenever I have to turn my camera on for my online classes. It's just right there. And I know people tell me they don't see it, but... I don't believe them. When I'm in the CNC lab for work, I just feel like everyone is staring at it. Like they're noticing my crooked face before they even look at the work I'm doing. So, yeah, I think it really looks like that, and I'm definitely afraid of what they're thinking when they see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people would judge you or treat you differently if they saw your face as it really is?\n\nPatient: I mean, I don't know for sure if they'd treat me differently. I haven't really had anyone, like, say anything directly to my face or treat me bad at the shop or anything. \n\nBut... I definitely think they judge me. I just assume they're looking at me and thinking about how weird and crooked my face is. So I don't know exactly how it changes the way they act toward me, but I definitely feel like they're judging me for it in their heads.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check your face if you were completely alone?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think I would. I mean, even when I'm just getting ready in the morning, before anyone else is even around, I get stuck doing it. \n\nBefore I leave for school, I'll be in the bathroom just... checking my face in the mirror over and over. And then I'll use my phone camera to check it from different angles. I'll try fixing my hair differently or changing my shirt to see if it hides the crookedness better. I even ask my mom if it looks weird before I head out. Sometimes I get so caught up in doing all that, checking and trying to fix it, that I actually miss my lab entirely, or by the time I get there, I'm just too wiped out and stressed to even focus on my work. So yeah, it's not just when people are looking at me, it happens when I'm alone too.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still worry about your face if you were certain that no one else could see you or judge you?\n\nPatient: Honestly... yeah, I think I still would. \n\nEven if I knew for a fact that nobody else was ever going to see me or judge me, *I* still have to see it. I'd still know that my jaw and my nose are crooked. It just... it really bothers me that it looks so wrong. \n\nSo, I don't think the worry would just go away if other people weren't in the picture. I'm pretty sure I'd still be upset about it just because of how it looks to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel distressed about your face if you were certain that it actually looked perfectly normal to others, but you personally still saw it as crooked?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think I definitely would. I mean, even if you told me everyone else thought I looked fine, I'm the one who has to look in the mirror, you know? \n\nIf I still see my jaw and nose all messed up and crooked, that's what's going to bother me. It's not just about what other people think. It's about the fact that I can see it, and it feels so wrong to me. So, yeah, I'd still be really upset and stressed out about it, even if everyone else said it was normal.\n\nDoctor: Is it the fact that you think your face looks crooked that bothers you, or is it more the fear of what others might think when they see you?\n\nPatient: It's kind of both, to be honest. I mean, the main reason I came in is because I just can’t stop thinking about how the left side of my face looks off. Whenever I see an ID photo or look at my camera for an online class, my nose and jaw just look so crooked to me, and that bothers me a lot on its own. \n\nBut the fear of what other people think is definitely a huge part of it, too. Even when people tell me they don’t see it, I always feel like everyone in the CNC lab is noticing my face before they even notice my work. So... the crookedness itself really upsets me, but feeling like everyone else is staring at it just makes it impossible to ignore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking behaviors are trying to make your face look perfect or exactly symmetrical?\n\nPatient: I don't know if I'd say I'm trying to make it perfect. It's more like... I'm just trying to hide it, or make it look less wrong. \n\nLike, before I leave for school, I get completely stuck checking my face in the bathroom mirror, and then I'll check my phone camera, too. I'll keep fixing my hair or changing my shirts just to try and hide the angle. I'll even ask my mom if it looks weird. I just get stuck doing it for so long that sometimes I actually miss my lab entirely. Or, if I do make it, I show up so wiped out from all the checking that I can't even focus on my work.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to fix a specific flaw, like the crookedness you mentioned, or do you want your face to look flawless in general?\n\nPatient: Um, it's really just that specific thing. I'm not trying to look like a model or have a flawless face in general. I just... I just want the left side of my face to not look so messed up. \n\nIf my jaw and nose just looked normal, I wouldn't be doing all this checking and hiding. So, yeah, it's definitely about that one specific flaw, not about being perfect overall.\n\nDoctor: Is it the idea of having a flawed face that distresses you, or is it the thought of others evaluating you based on your appearance?\n\nPatient: I think... well, like I said earlier, it's really both. I guess the idea of having this flaw on my face is what starts the whole thing. It just bothers me so much to look at it and know it's there. \n\nBut the distress definitely gets worse when I think about other people looking at me. Like, I know they're evaluating me when they see my face. So it's the flaw itself that upsets me, but the thought of everyone else judging me for it is what makes it so hard to deal with.\n\nDoctor: Is it the fact that you believe your face is flawed that causes you distress, or is it the fear that others will negatively evaluate you because of your appearance?\n\nPatient: I mean... I think I just answered that, didn't I? It's really both things. The flaw itself bothers me a lot, just knowing it's there. But the fear of other people judging me for it is definitely what makes the distress so bad. So, yeah, it's both.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your face would improve if you could somehow make the perceived flaw go away, or would you likely find something else about your appearance to worry about?\n\nPatient: I... I really don't know. I guess I haven't thought about it that way. \n\nRight now, I'm just so focused on this one specific issue with my jaw and nose. I'd like to think that if it was just gone tomorrow, I'd be completely fine and could just live my life normally again. But honestly... I'm not sure. I can't say for certain if I'd end up finding something else to worry about instead. I just know how bad this one thing feels right now.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. So, yeah, I'd still be really upset and stressed out about it, even if everyone else said it was normal.\n\nDoctor: Is it the fact that you think your face looks crooked that bothers you, or is it more the fear of what others might think when they see you?\n\nPatient: It's kind of both, to be honest. I mean, the main reason I came in is because I just can’t stop thinking about how the left side of my face looks off. So... the crookedness itself really upsets me, but feeling like everyone else is staring at it just makes it impossible to ignore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking behaviors are trying to make your face look perfect or exactly symmetrical?\n\nPatient: I don't know if I'd say I'm trying to make it perfect. It's more like... So, yeah, it's definitely about that one specific flaw, not about being perfect overall.\n\nDoctor: Is it the idea of having a flawed face that distresses you, or is it the thought of others evaluating you based on your appearance?\n\nPatient: I think... well, like I said earlier, it's really both. But the fear of other people judging me for it is definitely what makes the distress so bad. So, yeah, it's both.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your face would improve if you could somehow make the perceived flaw go away, or would you likely find something else about your appearance to worry about?\n\nPatient: I...
